I guess I came in because I’ve been feeling… more thrown off than I expected lately. I’ve been trying to clean up my image and my routine because of some arts board stuff I’m involved with, and I thought that would make me feel more organized, or at least more together. But instead I’ve just been really tense and kind of down about it.

I’m still doing the basics, like I’m not completely falling apart or anything, but I keep dropping these smaller things that I normally care about. Like fair planning notes, or design drafts I meant to finish or revise, and then when I realize I’ve let something slip, I feel guilty and embarrassed. And instead of just catching up like a normal person, I end up canceling plans or avoiding people because I feel behind and don’t want to deal with it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s not like it’s every single second of the day, but it’s been happening a lot more than I’m used to. It sort of comes in waves, I guess. Like, if I’m just getting through basic stuff, I can seem okay, and I can still do the minimum things I need to do. But then I’ll remember something small I let slide, like notes for the fair planning or a design draft I meant to clean up, and it just sinks my mood.

And then it kind of sticks for a while because I start feeling guilty and embarrassed, like I should be handling things better, especially since the whole point was to get my image and routine together for the arts board stuff. Instead, I feel tense and disappointed in myself. So I’ll end up canceling plans or avoiding people because I feel behind, and that makes me feel even worse.

So I wouldn’t say I’m completely depressed all day, nonstop, but it’s been frequent enough that it feels like it’s hanging around in the background most days lately.

Instead, I feel tense and disappointed in myself. So I’ll end up canceling plans or avoiding people because I feel behind, and that makes me feel even worse.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m completely depressed all day, nonstop, but it’s been frequent enough that it feels like it’s hanging around in the background most days lately.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think it’s like I’ve stopped liking art or the board planning, because I still care about those things a lot. Sometimes maybe too much, which is part of why I get so worked up when I feel like I’m not doing them right.

It’s more like… when I sit down to do something I normally enjoy, I’m already thinking about what I’m behind on or how it’s going to look to other people, so it doesn’t feel as easy or fun in the moment. But I wouldn’t say I have no interest in it. I still want to do the drafts, and I still want the planning to go well. It’s just been harder to relax into it, if that makes sense.

Sometimes maybe too much, which is part of why I get so worked up when I feel like I’m not doing them right.\n\nIt’s more like… when I sit down to do something I normally enjoy, I’m already thinking about what I’m behind on or how it’s going to look to other people, so it doesn’t feel as easy or fun in the moment. But I wouldn’t say I have no interest in it. I still want to do the drafts, and I still want the planning to go well. It’s just been harder to relax into it, if that makes sense.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed that you're feeling more tired or run down lately, even if you're not doing anything particularly strenuous?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 23 / not_in_workforce / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. I’m not really sure. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed being physically exhausted in some big way, like where I can’t get through the day or anything. It’s more that I feel kind of mentally worn out from thinking about everything, if that makes sense.

Yeah, sort of, but I don’t think it’s that they feel completely uninteresting, exactly. I still care about them a lot, maybe that’s why it’s been bothering me so much. It’s more like since I started trying to get my image and routine together for the arts board stuff, everything has felt heavier than I expected.

Like, I’ll sit down meaning to work on fair planning notes or clean up a design draft, and instead of feeling excited or focused, I notice all the little things I’ve dropped or let slide. Then I start feeling guilty, like I should’ve already handled it, and embarrassed that I’m not keeping up the way I wanted to. So the thing itself isn’t flat because I don’t care — it’s more like the stress gets in the way of enjoying it.

I’m still doing the basic things I need to do, but I keep missing these smaller things that actually matter to me, and then I get tense and down about it. Sometimes instead of catching up, I’ll cancel plans or avoid people because I feel behind, which just makes the whole thing feel worse. So… not uninteresting exactly, but definitely not enjoyable in the normal way.

It sort of comes in waves, I guess. Like, if I’m just getting through basic stuff, I can seem okay, and I can still do the minimum things I need to do. But then I’ll remember something small I let slide, like notes for the fair planning or a design draft I meant to clean up, and it just sinks my mood.\n\nAnd then it kind of sticks for a while because I start feeling guilty and embarrassed, like I should be handling things better, especially since the whole point was to get my image and routine together for the arts board stuff. Instead, I feel tense and disappointed in myself. So I’ll end up canceling plans or avoiding people because I feel behind, and that makes me feel even worse.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m completely depressed all day, nonstop, but it’s been frequent enough that it feels like it’s hanging around in the background most days lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not getting as much enjoyment or interest out of things you usually like doing, like your art or planning for the board?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think it’s like I’ve stopped liking art or the board planning, because I still care about those things a lot. Sometimes maybe too much, which is part of why I get so worked up when I feel like I’m not doing them right.\n\nIt’s more like… when I sit down to do something I normally enjoy, I’m already thinking about what I’m behind on or how it’s going to look to other people, so it doesn’t feel as easy or fun in the moment. But I wouldn’t say I have no interest in it. I still want to do the drafts, and I still want the planning to go well. It’s just been harder to relax into it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're feeling more tired or run down lately, even if you're not doing anything particularly strenuous?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed being physically exhausted in some big way, like where I can’t get through the day or anything. It’s more that I feel kind of mentally worn out from thinking about everything, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll be sitting there not doing anything that demanding, but my head is still going over what I forgot, what I should be fixing, whether I’m making a bad impression, and that can make me feel sort of drained. But I don’t know if that’s the same as being tired or run down in the way you mean. I’m still able to do basic things, so it’s not like I’m constantly wiped out. It’s just… I feel worn out by the stress of it, more than by actual activity.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that activities you usually enjoy, like your art or planning for the board, feel flat or uninteresting, even if you still care about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sort of, but I don’t think it’s that they feel completely uninteresting, exactly. I still care about them a lot, maybe that’s why it’s been bothering me so much. It’s more like since I started trying to get my image and routine together for the arts board stuff, everything has felt heavier than I expected.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down meaning to work on fair planning notes or clean up a design draft, and instead of feeling excited or focused, I notice all the little things I’ve dropped or let slide. Yeah… I can think of little moments, I guess. Like if I’m actually in the middle of a design draft and I stop thinking about whether I’m behind or whether it’s good enough, there are still parts where I’m like, “Oh, that looks right,” or I get interested in fixing one small piece of it. So it’s not like that feeling is totally gone.

But it doesn’t last very long lately. Since I started trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I’ve been more tense and kind of down than I expected. I’ll start out trying to work on fair planning notes or a draft, and then I notice some little thing I dropped that I normally would’ve cared about, and it kind of ruins the good feeling. Then I feel guilty, like I should have already handled it, and instead of catching up I’ll sometimes cancel plans or avoid dealing with it for a bit.

So yes, there are still small moments where I feel interested or pleased, but they get crowded out pretty quickly by the stress around it.

Not exactly like I don’t care at all, no. That’s the frustrating part — I do still care about my art and the board planning. I think if I genuinely didn’t care, it might almost feel simpler, but I do care, and then I feel worse when I’m not keeping up with it the way I think I should.

What brought me in is more that since I started trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I’ve felt more tense and down than I expected. I’m still doing the basic things, like I’m not just completely abandoning everything, but I keep dropping these smaller things that I normally would stay on top of — fair planning notes, design drafts, little follow-ups or pieces of prep — and then I get this guilty feeling, like, “Why didn’t I just handle that?”

And then sometimes instead of catching up, I cancel plans or avoid people because I feel behind or embarrassed. So it’s not that I don’t want the art or the board work at all. It’s more like wanting to do it is still there, but the stress around it makes me pull back or freeze up.

Sometimes instead of catching up, I’ll cancel plans or avoid people because I feel behind, which just makes the whole thing feel worse. So… not uninteresting exactly, but definitely not enjoyable in the normal way.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you felt genuinely interested or pleased while working on your art or the board planning, even for a little while?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I can think of little moments, I guess. Like if I’m actually in the middle of a design draft and I stop thinking about whether I’m behind or whether it’s good enough, there are still parts where I’m like, “Oh, that looks right,” or I get interested in fixing one small piece of it. It’s not like I’m pacing all day or can’t sit still at all. And I don’t think I’m speaking slowly or anything like that.\n\nSo I guess my answer is mostly no, or at least I haven’t noticed a clear change there.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself not caring about things you usually care about, even your art or the planning for the board, not even wanting to do them at all?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like I don’t care at all, no. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think they do feel less satisfying, but not because I stopped caring about them. It’s more like the enjoyment gets mixed up with pressure now.

Like, before, if I was working on a design draft or making notes for fair planning, I could kind of get absorbed in it and feel like, okay, this is why I like doing this. Lately, since I’ve been trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I go into it already feeling tense, like I’m trying to prove I can keep everything under control. So even when I do the basic things, there’s this background stress.

And then I’ll notice I dropped some small thing I normally would care about — like a planning note I meant to finish, or a draft I should’ve revised — and instead of the work feeling satisfying, it turns into guilt. I start thinking, “Why didn’t I just handle that earlier?” Then sometimes I’ll cancel plans or avoid catching up because I feel embarrassed or behind, which obviously makes it worse.

So, yes, I still care about the art and the board planning, but they don’t feel as cleanly enjoyable as they used to. They feel heavier right now.

Like fair planning notes, or design drafts I meant to finish or revise, and then when I realize I’ve let something slip, I feel guilty and embarrassed. And instead of just catching up like a normal person, I end up canceling plans or avoiding people because I feel behind and don’t want to deal with it. It’s just been this loop, and I don’t really like how I’m handling it.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how often you’ve felt down or depressed lately, and whether this feeling seems to come and go or stays with you most of the time?\n\nPatient: It’s not like it’s every single second of the day, but it’s been happening a lot more than I’m used to. It sort of comes in waves, I guess. Like, if I’m just getting through basic stuff, I can seem okay, and I can still do the minimum things I need to do. But then I’ll remember something small I let slide, like notes for the fair planning or a design draft I meant to clean up, and it just sinks my mood.\n\nAnd then it kind of sticks for a while because I start feeling guilty and embarrassed, like I should be handling things better, especially since the whole point was to get my image and routine together for the arts board stuff. Instead, I feel tense and disappointed in myself. So I’ll end up canceling plans or avoiding people because I feel behind, and that makes me feel even worse.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m completely depressed all day, nonstop, but it’s been frequent enough that it feels like it’s hanging around in the background most days lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not getting as much enjoyment or interest out of things you usually like doing, like your art or planning for the board?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t think it’s like I’ve stopped liking art or the board planning, because I still care about those things a lot. Sometimes maybe too much, which is part of why I get so worked up when I feel like I’m not doing them right.\n\nIt’s more like… when I sit down to do something I normally enjoy, I’m already thinking about what I’m behind on or how it’s going to look to other people, so it doesn’t feel as easy or fun in the moment. But I wouldn’t say I have no interest in it. I still want to do the drafts, and I still want the planning to go well. It’s just been harder to relax into it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're feeling more tired or run down lately, even if you're not doing anything particularly strenuous?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed being physically exhausted in some big way, like where I can’t get through the day or anything. It’s more that I feel kind of mentally worn out from thinking about everything, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I’ll be sitting there not doing anything that demanding, but my head is still going over what I forgot, what I should be fixing, whether I’m making a bad impression, and that can make me feel sort of drained. But I don’t know if that’s the same as being tired or run down in the way you mean. I’m still able to do basic things, so it’s not like I’m constantly wiped out. It’s just… I feel worn out by the stress of it, more than by actual activity.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that activities you usually enjoy, like your art or planning for the board, feel flat or uninteresting, even if you still care about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sort of, but I don’t think it’s that they feel completely uninteresting, exactly. I still care about them a lot, maybe that’s why it’s been bothering me so much. It’s more like since I started trying to get my image and routine together for the arts board stuff, everything has felt heavier than I expected.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down meaning to work on fair planning notes or clean up a design draft, and instead of feeling excited or focused, I notice all the little things I’ve dropped or let slide. Then I start feeling guilty, like I should’ve already handled it, and embarrassed that I’m not keeping up the way I wanted to. So the thing itself isn’t flat because I don’t care — it’s more like the stress gets in the way of enjoying it.\n\nI’m still doing the basic things I need to do, but I keep missing these smaller things that actually matter to me, and then I get tense and down about it. Sometimes instead of catching up, I’ll cancel plans or avoid people because I feel behind, which just makes the whole thing feel worse. So… not uninteresting exactly, but definitely not enjoyable in the normal way.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you felt genuinely interested or pleased while working on your art or the board planning, even for a little while?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I can think of little moments, I guess. Like if I’m actually in the middle of a design draft and I stop thinking about whether I’m behind or whether it’s good enough, there are still parts where I’m like, “Oh, that looks right,” or I get interested in fixing one small piece of it. So it’s not like that feeling is totally gone.\n\nBut it doesn’t last very long lately. Since I started trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I’ve been more tense and kind of down than I expected. I’ll start out trying to work on fair planning notes or a draft, and then I notice some little thing I dropped that I normally would’ve cared about, and it kind of ruins the good feeling. Then I feel guilty, like I should have already handled it, and instead of catching up I’ll sometimes cancel plans or avoid dealing with it for a bit.\n\nSo yes, there are still small moments where I feel interested or pleased, but they get crowded out pretty quickly by the stress around it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling restless or fidgety in a way that’s not typical for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a really obvious way. I haven’t had anyone tell me I’m talking slower or moving strangely, and I don’t feel like I’m dragging physically, if that’s what you mean.\n\nRestless… maybe a little in the sense that I can have trouble just settling down with things, like I’ll shift around or get up and check something, but I’m not sure that’s unusual for me or if I’m just noticing it more because I’m already stressed about how I’m handling everything. It’s not like I’m pacing all day or can’t sit still at all. And I don’t think I’m speaking slowly or anything like that.\n\nSo I guess my answer is mostly no, or at least I haven’t noticed a clear change there.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself not caring about things you usually care about, even your art or the planning for the board, not even wanting to do them at all?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like I don’t care at all, no. That’s the frustrating part — I do still care about my art and the board planning. I think if I genuinely didn’t care, it might almost feel simpler, but I do care, and then I feel worse when I’m not keeping up with it the way I think I should.\n\nWhat brought me in is more that since I started trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I’ve felt more tense and down than I expected. I’m still doing the basic things, like I’m not just completely abandoning everything, but I keep dropping these smaller things that I normally would stay on top of — fair planning notes, design drafts, little follow-ups or pieces of prep — and then I get this guilty feeling, like, “Why didn’t I just handle that?”\n\nAnd then sometimes instead of catching up, I cancel plans or avoid people because I feel behind or embarrassed. So it’s not that I don’t want the art or the board work at all. It’s more like wanting to do it is still there, but the stress around it makes me pull back or freeze up.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you’ve noticed a change in how you feel about doing your art or working on the board planning — like, even if you still care about them, do they feel less satisfying or enjoyable than they used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think they do feel less satisfying, but not because I stopped caring about them. It’s more like the enjoyment gets mixed up with pressure now.\n\nLike, before, if I was working on a design draft or making notes for fair planning, I could kind of get absorbed in it and feel like, okay, this is why I like doing this. Lately, since I’ve been trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I go into it already feeling tense, like I’m trying to prove I can keep everything under control. So even when I do the basic things, there’s this background stress.\n\nAnd then I’ll notice I dropped some small thing I normally would care about — like a planning note I meant to finish, or a draft I should’ve revised — and instead of the work feeling satisfying, it turns into guilt. I start thinking, “Why didn’t I just handle that earlier?” Then sometimes I’ll cancel plans or avoid catching up because I feel embarrassed or behind, which obviously makes it worse.\n\nSo, yes, I still care about the art and the board planning, but they don’t feel as cleanly enjoyable as they used to. They feel heavier right now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It’s more like the enjoyment gets mixed up with pressure now.\n\nLike, before, if I was working on a design draft or making notes for fair planning, I could kind of get absorbed in it and feel like, okay, this is why I like doing this. Lately, since I’ve been trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I go into it already feeling tense, like I’m trying to prove I can keep everything under control. So even when I do the basic things, there’s this background stress.\n\nAnd then I’ll notice I dropped some small thing I normally would care about — like a planning note I meant to finish, or a draft I should’ve revised — and instead of the work feeling satisfying, it turns into guilt. I start thinking, “Why didn’t I just handle that earlier?” Then sometimes I’ll cancel plans or avoid catching up because I feel embarrassed or behind, which obviously makes it worse.\n\nSo, yes, I still care about the art and the board planning, but they don’t feel as cleanly enjoyable as they used to. They feel heavier right now.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas there been a time in the last few weeks when something you used to really enjoy, like your art or the board planning, didn’t feel good or interesting to you at all, even for a little while?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 23 / not_in_workforce / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I can point to a moment where it felt like I had no interest at all. That sounds a little stronger than what I’ve noticed.

There have definitely been times where I sat down to do something art-related or board-planning-related and it just didn’t feel good in the way it normally does. Like I’d open up a draft or look at planning notes and instead of feeling curious or excited, I’d feel kind of tired of it before I even started — not physically tired exactly, just like, “Ugh, I don’t want to deal with this right now.” But even then, I don’t think it was that I didn’t care or that it had no meaning to me. It was more that I was already tense about it, so the interest got buried under that.

So… maybe yes in small patches, if you mean it didn’t feel enjoyable for a little while. But if you mean completely blank, like I felt nothing toward it or didn’t care whether I ever did it again, then no, I don’t think that’s happened. It’s more that it feels heavy and pressured, not totally uninteresting.

stressor or its consequences\n  manifestation: The whole reset is still going on, so this has not been some separate problem continuing long after the changes ended.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I am still getting through basic errands and commitments, but I have been less reliable in the volunteer and creative roles people usually count on me for, like fair planning and design follow-through.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about two months, since I started trying to overhaul my routine and presentation.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 23 / not_in_workforce / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S081\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you found yourself not caring about things you usually care about, even your art or the planning for the board, not even wanting to do them at all?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like I don’t care at all, no. That’s the frustrating part — I do still care about my art and the board planning. I think if I genuinely didn’t care, it might almost feel simpler, but I do care, and then I feel worse when I’m not keeping up with it the way I think I should.\n\nWhat brought me in is more that since I started trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I’ve felt more tense and down than I expected. I’m still doing the basic things, like I’m not just completely abandoning everything, but I keep dropping these smaller things that I normally would stay on top of — fair planning notes, design drafts, little follow-ups or pieces of prep — and then I get this guilty feeling, like, “Why didn’t I just handle that?”\n\nAnd then sometimes instead of catching up, I cancel plans or avoid people because I feel behind or embarrassed. So it’s not that I don’t want the art or the board work at all. It’s more like wanting to do it is still there, but the stress around it makes me pull back or freeze up.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you’ve noticed a change in how you feel about doing your art or working on the board planning — like, even if you still care about them, do they feel less satisfying or enjoyable than they used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think they do feel less satisfying, but not because I stopped caring about them. It’s more like the enjoyment gets mixed up with pressure now.\n\nLike, before, if I was working on a design draft or making notes for fair planning, I could kind of get absorbed in it and feel like, okay, this is why I like doing this. So even when I do the basic things, there’s this background stress.\n\nAnd then I’ll notice I dropped some small thing I normally would care about — like a planning note I meant to finish, or a draft I should’ve revised — and instead of the work feeling satisfying, it turns into guilt. I start thinking, “Why didn’t I just handle that earlier?” Then sometimes I’ll cancel plans or avoid catching up because I feel embarrassed or behind, which obviously makes it worse.\n\nSo, yes, I still care about the art and the board planning, but they don’t feel as cleanly enjoyable as they used to. They feel heavier right now.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time in the last few weeks when something you used to really enjoy, like your art or the board planning, didn’t feel good or interesting to you at all, even for a little while?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to a moment where it felt like I had no interest at all. That sounds a little stronger than what I’ve noticed.\n\nThere have definitely been times where I sat down to do something art-related or board-planning-related and it just didn’t feel good in the way it normally does. Like I’d open up a draft or look at planning notes and instead of feeling curious or excited, I’d feel kind of tired of it before I even started — not physically tired exactly, just like, “Ugh, I don’t want to deal with this right now.” But even then, I don’t think it was that I didn’t care or that it had no meaning to me. It was more that I was already tense about it, so the interest got buried under that.\n\nSo… maybe yes in small patches, if you mean it didn’t feel enjoyable for a little while. But if you mean completely blank, like I felt nothing toward it or didn’t care whether I ever did it again, then no, I don’t think that’s happened. It’s more that it feels heavy and pressured, not totally uninteresting.\n\n[Last Question]\nIn the past few weeks, have there been times when you couldn’t feel any pleasure or interest in things, even things you usually enjoy like your art or the board planning — like nothing felt good or meaningful at all?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I’ve had it where nothing felt good or meaningful at all. That sounds more absolute than what I’ve been experiencing.

It’s more like… things can feel dulled or weighed down, especially with the art and board planning, but there’s still something there. Like even if I’m frustrated or tense, I still know I care about it, and sometimes I can still get a little satisfaction from doing part of it, even if it’s not the same as before. I wouldn’t say it’s this total blankness where I can’t feel any pleasure in anything.

So, no, not really “nothing felt good or meaningful at all.” It’s more that the good feeling is harder to get to, if that makes sense.

It’s more like since I started trying to get my image and routine together for the arts board stuff, everything has felt heavier than I expected.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down meaning to work on fair planning notes or clean up a design draft, and instead of feeling excited or focused, I notice all the little things I’ve dropped or let slide. Then I feel guilty, like I should have already handled it, and instead of catching up I’ll sometimes cancel plans or avoid dealing with it for a bit.\n\nSo yes, there are still small moments where I feel interested or pleased, but they get crowded out pretty quickly by the stress around it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling restless or fidgety in a way that’s not typical for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a really obvious way. But I don’t know if that’s the same as being tired or run down in the way you mean. I’m still able to do basic things, so it’s not like I’m constantly wiped out. It’s just… I feel worn out by the stress of it, more than by actual activity.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that activities you usually enjoy, like your art or planning for the board, feel flat or uninteresting, even if you still care about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sort of, but I don’t think it’s that they feel completely uninteresting, exactly. I still care about them a lot, maybe that’s why it’s been bothering me so much. It’s more like since I started trying to get my image and routine together for the arts board stuff, everything has felt heavier than I expected.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down meaning to work on fair planning notes or clean up a design draft, and instead of feeling excited or focused, I notice all the little things I’ve dropped or let slide. Then I start feeling guilty, like I should’ve already handled it, and embarrassed that I’m not keeping up the way I wanted to. So the thing itself isn’t flat because I don’t care — it’s more like the stress gets in the way of enjoying it.\n\nI’m still doing the basic things I need to do, but I keep missing these smaller things that actually matter to me, and then I get tense and down about it. Sometimes instead of catching up, I’ll cancel plans or avoid people because I feel behind, which just makes the whole thing feel worse. So… not uninteresting exactly, but definitely not enjoyable in the normal way.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you felt genuinely interested or pleased while working on your art or the board planning, even for a little while?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I can think of little moments, I guess. Like if I’m actually in the middle of a design draft and I stop thinking about whether I’m behind or whether it’s good enough, there are still parts where I’m like, “Oh, that looks right,” or I get interested in fixing one small piece of it. So it’s not like that feeling is totally gone.\n\nBut it doesn’t last very long lately. Since I started trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I’ve been more tense and kind of down than I expected. I’ll start out trying to work on fair planning notes or a draft, and then I notice some little thing I dropped that I normally would’ve cared about, and it kind of ruins the good feeling. Then I feel guilty, like I should have already handled it, and instead of catching up I’ll sometimes cancel plans or avoid dealing with it for a bit.\n\nSo yes, there are still small moments where I feel interested or pleased, but they get crowded out pretty quickly by the stress around it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling restless or fidgety in a way that’s not typical for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a really obvious way. I haven’t had anyone tell me I’m talking slower or moving strangely, and I don’t feel like I’m dragging physically, if that’s what you mean.\n\nRestless… maybe a little in the sense that I can have trouble just settling down with things, like I’ll shift around or get up and check something, but I’m not sure that’s unusual for me or if I’m just noticing it more because I’m already stressed about how I’m handling everything. It’s not like I’m pacing all day or can’t sit still at all. And I don’t think I’m speaking slowly or anything like that.\n\nSo I guess my answer is mostly no, or at least I haven’t noticed a clear change there.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself not caring about things you usually care about, even your art or the planning for the board, not even wanting to do them at all?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like I don’t care at all, no. That’s the frustrating part — I do still care about my art and the board planning. I think if I genuinely didn’t care, it might almost feel simpler, but I do care, and then I feel worse when I’m not keeping up with it the way I think I should.\n\nWhat brought me in is more that since I started trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I’ve felt more tense and down than I expected. I’m still doing the basic things, like I’m not just completely abandoning everything, but I keep dropping these smaller things that I normally would stay on top of — fair planning notes, design drafts, little follow-ups or pieces of prep — and then I get this guilty feeling, like, “Why didn’t I just handle that?”\n\nAnd then sometimes instead of catching up, I cancel plans or avoid people because I feel behind or embarrassed. So it’s not that I don’t want the art or the board work at all. It’s more like wanting to do it is still there, but the stress around it makes me pull back or freeze up.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you’ve noticed a change in how you feel about doing your art or working on the board planning — like, even if you still care about them, do they feel less satisfying or enjoyable than they used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think they do feel less satisfying, but not because I stopped caring about them. It’s more like the enjoyment gets mixed up with pressure now.\n\nLike, before, if I was working on a design draft or making notes for fair planning, I could kind of get absorbed in it and feel like, okay, this is why I like doing this. Lately, since I’ve been trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I go into it already feeling tense, like I’m trying to prove I can keep everything under control. So even when I do the basic things, there’s this background stress.\n\nAnd then I’ll notice I dropped some small thing I normally would care about — like a planning note I meant to finish, or a draft I should’ve revised — and instead of the work feeling satisfying, it turns into guilt. Like I’d open up a draft or look at planning notes and instead of feeling curious or excited, I’d feel kind of tired of it before I even started — not physically tired exactly, just like, “Ugh, I don’t want to deal with this right now.” But even then, I don’t think it was that I didn’t care or that it had no meaning to me. It was more that I was already tense about it, so the interest got buried under that.\n\nSo… maybe yes in small patches, if you mean it didn’t feel enjoyable for a little while. It’s more that it feels heavy and pressured, not totally uninteresting.\n\nDoctor: In the past few weeks, have there been times when you couldn’t feel any pleasure or interest in things, even things you usually enjoy like your art or the board planning — like nothing felt good or meaningful at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had it where nothing felt good or meaningful at all. That sounds more absolute than what I’ve been experiencing.\n\nIt’s more like… things can feel dulled or weighed down, especially with the art and board planning, but there’s still something there. Like even if I’m frustrated or tense, I still know I care about it, and sometimes I can still get a little satisfaction from doing part of it, even if it’s not the same as before. I don’t think I’d call it a persistent loss of energy, no. I mean, I have moments where I feel kind of drained, but when I really think about it, it usually seems tied to being tense or overwhelmed by what I’m trying to handle, not like this constant fatigue that’s there no matter what.

I’ll start out trying to work on fair planning notes or a draft, and then I notice some little thing I dropped that I normally would’ve cared about, and it kind of ruins the good feeling. Then I feel guilty, like I should have already handled it, and instead of catching up I’ll sometimes cancel plans or avoid dealing with it for a bit.\n\nSo yes, there are still small moments where I feel interested or pleased, but they get crowded out pretty quickly by the stress around it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling restless or fidgety in a way that’s not typical for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a really obvious way. I haven’t had anyone tell me I’m talking slower or moving strangely, and I don’t feel like I’m dragging physically, if that’s what you mean.\n\nRestless… maybe a little in the sense that I can have trouble just settling down with things, like I’ll shift around or get up and check something, but I’m not sure that’s unusual for me or if I’m just noticing it more because I’m already stressed about how I’m handling everything. It’s not like I’m pacing all day or can’t sit still at all. And I don’t think I’m speaking slowly or anything like that.\n\nSo I guess my answer is mostly no, or at least I haven’t noticed a clear change there.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself not caring about things you usually care about, even your art or the planning for the board, not even wanting to do them at all?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like I don’t care at all, no. That’s the frustrating part — I do still care about my art and the board planning. I think if I genuinely didn’t care, it might almost feel simpler, but I do care, and then I feel worse when I’m not keeping up with it the way I think I should.\n\nWhat brought me in is more that since I started trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I’ve felt more tense and down than I expected. I’m still doing the basic things, like I’m not just completely abandoning everything, but I keep dropping these smaller things that I normally would stay on top of — fair planning notes, design drafts, little follow-ups or pieces of prep — and then I get this guilty feeling, like, “Why didn’t I just handle that?”\n\nAnd then sometimes instead of catching up, I cancel plans or avoid people because I feel behind or embarrassed. So it’s not that I don’t want the art or the board work at all. It’s more like wanting to do it is still there, but the stress around it makes me pull back or freeze up.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you’ve noticed a change in how you feel about doing your art or working on the board planning — like, even if you still care about them, do they feel less satisfying or enjoyable than they used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think they do feel less satisfying, but not because I stopped caring about them. It’s more like the enjoyment gets mixed up with pressure now.\n\nLike, before, if I was working on a design draft or making notes for fair planning, I could kind of get absorbed in it and feel like, okay, this is why I like doing this. Lately, since I’ve been trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I go into it already feeling tense, like I’m trying to prove I can keep everything under control. So even when I do the basic things, there’s this background stress.\n\nAnd then I’ll notice I dropped some small thing I normally would care about — like a planning note I meant to finish, or a draft I should’ve revised — and instead of the work feeling satisfying, it turns into guilt. I start thinking, “Why didn’t I just handle that earlier?” Then sometimes I’ll cancel plans or avoid catching up because I feel embarrassed or behind, which obviously makes it worse.\n\nSo, yes, I still care about the art and the board planning, but they don’t feel as cleanly enjoyable as they used to. They feel heavier right now.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time in the last few weeks when something you used to really enjoy, like your art or the board planning, didn’t feel good or interesting to you at all, even for a little while?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to a moment where it felt like I had no interest at all. It was more that I was already tense about it, so the interest got buried under that.\n\nSo… maybe yes in small patches, if you mean it didn’t feel enjoyable for a little while. But if you mean completely blank, like I felt nothing toward it or didn’t care whether I ever did it again, then no, I don’t think that’s happened. That sounds more absolute than what I’ve been experiencing.\n\nIt’s more like… things can feel dulled or weighed down, especially with the art and board planning, but there’s still something there. Like even if I’m frustrated or tense, I still know I care about it, and sometimes I can still get a little satisfaction from doing part of it, even if it’s not the same as before. I wouldn’t say it’s this total blankness where I can’t feel any pleasure in anything.\n\nSo, no, not really “nothing felt good or meaningful at all.” It’s more that the good feeling is harder to get to, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a persistent loss of energy or felt fatigued even when you haven’t been doing anything physically or mentally demanding?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it a persistent loss of energy, no. I mean, I have moments where I feel kind of drained, but when I really think about it, it usually seems tied to being tense or overwhelmed by what I’m trying to handle, not like this constant fatigue that’s there no matter what.\n\nLike, if I’m just sitting around and nothing much is happening, I don’t usually feel physically wiped out for no reason. It’s more that I can feel mentally sort of weighed down when I’m thinking about everything I should be doing, and then I might move slower or put things off. But I’m not sure that’s the same as having no energy even when nothing demanding is going on.\n\nSo… not really in a persistent way. I haven’t noticed that as a main thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like even if I’m frustrated or tense, I still know I care about it, and sometimes I can still get a little satisfaction from doing part of it, even if it’s not the same as before. I wouldn’t say it’s this total blankness where I can’t feel any pleasure in anything.\n\nSo, no, not really “nothing felt good or meaningful at all.” It’s more that the good feeling is harder to get to, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a persistent loss of energy or felt fatigued even when you haven’t been doing anything physically or mentally demanding?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it a persistent loss of energy, no. I mean, I have moments where I feel kind of drained, but when I really think about it, it usually seems tied to being tense or overwhelmed by what I’m trying to handle, not like this constant fatigue that’s there no matter what.\n\nLike, if I’m just sitting around and nothing much is happening, I don’t usually feel physically wiped out for no reason. It’s more that I can feel mentally sort of weighed down when I’m thinking about everything I should be doing, and then I might move slower or put things off. But I’m not sure that’s the same as having no energy even when nothing demanding is going on.\n\nSo… not really in a persistent way. I haven’t noticed that as a main thing.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been doing less of your usual activities, like your art or spending time with people, because you just don’t feel like it or it doesn’t seem worth the effort?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 23 / not_in_workforce / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response\nmanifestation: \"I came in because since I tried to clean up my image and routine for the arts board stuff, I’ve been more tense and down than I expected, like I’m still doing the basics but I keep dropping little things I normally care about, like fair planning notes or design drafts, and then I feel guilty and cancel plans instead of catching up.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think that’s actually closer to what’s been happening. Not like I’ve stopped everything or I’m just lying around doing nothing, because I am still doing the basics. I’m getting through the necessary stuff. But I’ve definitely been doing less of the smaller things I usually keep up with, especially around the arts board work.

Like, since I started trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for that, I expected to feel more organized or proud of myself, but instead I’ve been more tense and kind of down than I thought I would be. And then I’ll avoid little things that normally matter to me — fair planning notes, design drafts, things like that. They’re not impossible tasks, but they start to feel like this whole pile of pressure, and then I don’t do them, or I do the absolute minimum.

And with people… yeah, sometimes I cancel plans, but it’s not exactly because I don’t care about seeing them. It’s more like I feel guilty that I’m behind, and then instead of catching up or letting myself go out, I just cancel because I feel like I shouldn’t be doing anything fun until I’ve fixed everything. Which doesn’t really help, because then I’m alone and still not catching up. So yes, I guess I have been doing less, but it feels more tied to stress and guilt than just not caring.

But then I’ll remember something small I let slide, like notes for the fair planning or a design draft I meant to clean up, and it just sinks my mood.\n\nAnd then it kind of sticks for a while because I start feeling guilty and embarrassed, like I should be handling things better, especially since the whole point was to get my image and routine together for the arts board stuff. Sometimes maybe too much, which is part of why I get so worked up when I feel like I’m not doing them right.\n\nIt’s more like… when I sit down to do something I normally enjoy, I’m already thinking about what I’m behind on or how it’s going to look to other people, so it doesn’t feel as easy or fun in the moment. But I wouldn’t say I have no interest in it. I still want to do the drafts, and I still want the planning to go well. It’s just been harder to relax into it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're feeling more tired or run down lately, even if you're not doing anything particularly strenuous?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. It’s just… I feel worn out by the stress of it, more than by actual activity.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that activities you usually enjoy, like your art or planning for the board, feel flat or uninteresting, even if you still care about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sort of, but I don’t think it’s that they feel completely uninteresting, exactly. I still care about them a lot, maybe that’s why it’s been bothering me so much. It’s more like since I started trying to get my image and routine together for the arts board stuff, everything has felt heavier than I expected.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down meaning to work on fair planning notes or clean up a design draft, and instead of feeling excited or focused, I notice all the little things I’ve dropped or let slide. So… not uninteresting exactly, but definitely not enjoyable in the normal way.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you felt genuinely interested or pleased while working on your art or the board planning, even for a little while?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I can think of little moments, I guess. Like if I’m actually in the middle of a design draft and I stop thinking about whether I’m behind or whether it’s good enough, there are still parts where I’m like, “Oh, that looks right,” or I get interested in fixing one small piece of it. So it’s not like that feeling is totally gone.\n\nBut it doesn’t last very long lately. Since I started trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I’ve been more tense and kind of down than I expected. I’ll start out trying to work on fair planning notes or a draft, and then I notice some little thing I dropped that I normally would’ve cared about, and it kind of ruins the good feeling. Then I feel guilty, like I should have already handled it, and instead of catching up I’ll sometimes cancel plans or avoid dealing with it for a bit.\n\nSo yes, there are still small moments where I feel interested or pleased, but they get crowded out pretty quickly by the stress around it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling restless or fidgety in a way that’s not typical for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a really obvious way. I haven’t had anyone tell me I’m talking slower or moving strangely, and I don’t feel like I’m dragging physically, if that’s what you mean.\n\nRestless… maybe a little in the sense that I can have trouble just settling down with things, like I’ll shift around or get up and check something, but I’m not sure that’s unusual for me or if I’m just noticing it more because I’m already stressed about how I’m handling everything. It’s not like I’m pacing all day or can’t sit still at all. And I don’t think I’m speaking slowly or anything like that.\n\nSo I guess my answer is mostly no, or at least I haven’t noticed a clear change there.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself not caring about things you usually care about, even your art or the planning for the board, not even wanting to do them at all?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like I don’t care at all, no. That’s the frustrating part — I do still care about my art and the board planning. I think if I genuinely didn’t care, it might almost feel simpler, but I do care, and then I feel worse when I’m not keeping up with it the way I think I should.\n\nWhat brought me in is more that since I started trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I’ve felt more tense and down than I expected. I’m still doing the basic things, like I’m not just completely abandoning everything, but I keep dropping these smaller things that I normally would stay on top of — fair planning notes, design drafts, little follow-ups or pieces of prep — and then I get this guilty feeling, like, “Why didn’t I just handle that?”\n\nAnd then sometimes instead of catching up, I cancel plans or avoid people because I feel behind or embarrassed. So it’s not that I don’t want the art or the board work at all. It’s more like wanting to do it is still there, but the stress around it makes me pull back or freeze up.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you’ve noticed a change in how you feel about doing your art or working on the board planning — like, even if you still care about them, do they feel less satisfying or enjoyable than they used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think they do feel less satisfying, but not because I stopped caring about them. It’s more like the enjoyment gets mixed up with pressure now.\n\nLike, before, if I was working on a design draft or making notes for fair planning, I could kind of get absorbed in it and feel like, okay, this is why I like doing this. Lately, since I’ve been trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I go into it already feeling tense, like I’m trying to prove I can keep everything under control. So even when I do the basic things, there’s this background stress.\n\nAnd then I’ll notice I dropped some small thing I normally would care about — like a planning note I meant to finish, or a draft I should’ve revised — and instead of the work feeling satisfying, it turns into guilt. I start thinking, “Why didn’t I just handle that earlier?” Then sometimes I’ll cancel plans or avoid catching up because I feel embarrassed or behind, which obviously makes it worse.\n\nSo, yes, I still care about the art and the board planning, but they don’t feel as cleanly enjoyable as they used to. They feel heavier right now.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time in the last few weeks when something you used to really enjoy, like your art or the board planning, didn’t feel good or interesting to you at all, even for a little while?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to a moment where it felt like I had no interest at all. I start thinking, “Why didn’t I just handle that earlier?” Then sometimes I’ll cancel plans or avoid catching up because I feel embarrassed or behind, which obviously makes it worse.\n\nSo, yes, I still care about the art and the board planning, but they don’t feel as cleanly enjoyable as they used to. They feel heavier right now.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time in the last few weeks when something you used to really enjoy, like your art or the board planning, didn’t feel good or interesting to you at all, even for a little while?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to a moment where it felt like I had no interest at all. That sounds a little stronger than what I’ve noticed.\n\nThere have definitely been times where I sat down to do something art-related or board-planning-related and it just didn’t feel good in the way it normally does. Like I’d open up a draft or look at planning notes and instead of feeling curious or excited, I’d feel kind of tired of it before I even started — not physically tired exactly, just like, “Ugh, I don’t want to deal with this right now.” But even then, I don’t think it was that I didn’t care or that it had no meaning to me. It was more that I was already tense about it, so the interest got buried under that.\n\nSo… maybe yes in small patches, if you mean it didn’t feel enjoyable for a little while. But if you mean completely blank, like I felt nothing toward it or didn’t care whether I ever did it again, then no, I don’t think that’s happened. It’s more that it feels heavy and pressured, not totally uninteresting.\n\nDoctor: In the past few weeks, have there been times when you couldn’t feel any pleasure or interest in things, even things you usually enjoy like your art or the board planning — like nothing felt good or meaningful at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had it where nothing felt good or meaningful at all. It’s more that I can feel mentally sort of weighed down when I’m thinking about everything I should be doing, and then I might move slower or put things off. But I’m not sure that’s the same as having no energy even when nothing demanding is going on.\n\nSo… not really in a persistent way. I haven’t noticed that as a main thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing less of your usual activities, like your art or spending time with people, because you just don’t feel like it or it doesn’t seem worth the effort?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually closer to what’s been happening. Yes… that’s probably one of the main things, honestly. It’s not usually that the task itself is huge. It can be something small, like writing up fair planning notes or working on a design draft, things I normally would just sit down and do without making it into a whole ordeal.

But lately, since I’ve been trying to sort of clean up my image and get into a better routine for the arts board stuff, it’s like I expected that to make me feel more together, and instead I’ve felt more tense and kind of down about it. Then if I let one little thing slip, it doesn’t stay little in my head. It turns into, “Well, now I’m behind, and now people are going to notice, and now I should have done this days ago,” and then starting it feels worse because I’m already guilty.

So I’m still doing the basic things I have to do, but I keep dropping these smaller things that I actually do care about. And then I’ll do something like cancel plans because I feel like I don’t deserve to go out or I shouldn’t be having fun until I’ve caught up. But then I don’t always catch up anyway, so it’s just me sitting there feeling bad about both things. It’s frustrating because I know the task would probably take less time than all the avoiding, but I still get stuck.

It’s more like the enjoyment gets mixed up with pressure now.\n\nLike, before, if I was working on a design draft or making notes for fair planning, I could kind of get absorbed in it and feel like, okay, this is why I like doing this. Lately, since I’ve been trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for the arts board stuff, I go into it already feeling tense, like I’m trying to prove I can keep everything under control. So even when I do the basic things, there’s this background stress.\n\nAnd then I’ll notice I dropped some small thing I normally would care about — like a planning note I meant to finish, or a draft I should’ve revised — and instead of the work feeling satisfying, it turns into guilt. I start thinking, “Why didn’t I just handle that earlier?” Then sometimes I’ll cancel plans or avoid catching up because I feel embarrassed or behind, which obviously makes it worse.\n\nSo, yes, I still care about the art and the board planning, but they don’t feel as cleanly enjoyable as they used to. They feel heavier right now.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time in the last few weeks when something you used to really enjoy, like your art or the board planning, didn’t feel good or interesting to you at all, even for a little while?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to a moment where it felt like I had no interest at all. That sounds a little stronger than what I’ve noticed.\n\nThere have definitely been times where I sat down to do something art-related or board-planning-related and it just didn’t feel good in the way it normally does. Like I’d open up a draft or look at planning notes and instead of feeling curious or excited, I’d feel kind of tired of it before I even started — not physically tired exactly, just like, “Ugh, I don’t want to deal with this right now.” But even then, I don’t think it was that I didn’t care or that it had no meaning to me. It was more that I was already tense about it, so the interest got buried under that.\n\nSo… maybe yes in small patches, if you mean it didn’t feel enjoyable for a little while. But if you mean completely blank, like I felt nothing toward it or didn’t care whether I ever did it again, then no, I don’t think that’s happened. It’s more that it feels heavy and pressured, not totally uninteresting.\n\nDoctor: In the past few weeks, have there been times when you couldn’t feel any pleasure or interest in things, even things you usually enjoy like your art or the board planning — like nothing felt good or meaningful at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had it where nothing felt good or meaningful at all. That sounds more absolute than what I’ve been experiencing.\n\nIt’s more like… things can feel dulled or weighed down, especially with the art and board planning, but there’s still something there. Like even if I’m frustrated or tense, I still know I care about it, and sometimes I can still get a little satisfaction from doing part of it, even if it’s not the same as before. I wouldn’t say it’s this total blankness where I can’t feel any pleasure in anything.\n\nSo, no, not really “nothing felt good or meaningful at all.” It’s more that the good feeling is harder to get to, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a persistent loss of energy or felt fatigued even when you haven’t been doing anything physically or mentally demanding?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it a persistent loss of energy, no. I mean, I have moments where I feel kind of drained, but when I really think about it, it usually seems tied to being tense or overwhelmed by what I’m trying to handle, not like this constant fatigue that’s there no matter what.\n\nLike, if I’m just sitting around and nothing much is happening, I don’t usually feel physically wiped out for no reason. It’s more that I can feel mentally sort of weighed down when I’m thinking about everything I should be doing, and then I might move slower or put things off. But I’m not sure that’s the same as having no energy even when nothing demanding is going on.\n\nSo… not really in a persistent way. I haven’t noticed that as a main thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing less of your usual activities, like your art or spending time with people, because you just don’t feel like it or it doesn’t seem worth the effort?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually closer to what’s been happening. Not like I’ve stopped everything or I’m just lying around doing nothing, because I am still doing the basics. I’m getting through the necessary stuff. But I’ve definitely been doing less of the smaller things I usually keep up with, especially around the arts board work.\n\nLike, since I started trying to clean up my image and get my routine together for that, I expected to feel more organized or proud of myself, but instead I’ve been more tense and kind of down than I thought I would be. And then I’ll avoid little things that normally matter to me — fair planning notes, design drafts, things like that. They’re not impossible tasks, but they start to feel like this whole pile of pressure, and then I don’t do them, or I do the absolute minimum.\n\nAnd with people… yeah, sometimes I cancel plans, but it’s not exactly because I don’t care about seeing them. It’s more like I feel guilty that I’m behind, and then instead of catching up or letting myself go out, I just cancel because I feel like I shouldn’t be doing anything fun until I’ve fixed everything. Which doesn’t really help, because then I’m alone and still not catching up. So yes, I guess I have been doing less, but it feels more tied to stress and guilt than just not caring.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding starting or finishing tasks, even small ones you normally wouldn’t think twice about, because you feel overwhelmed by what you’ve already let slip or guilty about not doing them sooner?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s probably one of the main things, honestly. It’s not usually that the task itself is huge. It can be something small, like writing up fair planning notes or working on a design draft, things I normally would just sit down and do without making it into a whole ordeal.\n\nBut lately, since I’ve been trying to sort of clean up my image and get into a better routine for the arts board stuff, it’s like I expected that to make me feel more together, and instead I’ve felt more tense and kind of down about it. Then if I let one little thing slip, it doesn’t stay little in my head. It turns into, “Well, now I’m behind, and now people are going to notice, and now I should have done this days ago,” and then starting it feels worse because I’m already guilty.\n\nSo I’m still doing the basic things I have to do, but I keep dropping these smaller things that I actually do care about. And then I’ll do something like cancel plans because I feel like I don’t deserve to go out or I shouldn’t be having fun until I’ve caught up. But then I don’t always catch up anyway, so it’s just me sitting there feeling bad about both things. It’s frustrating because I know the task would probably take less time than all the avoiding, but I still get stuck.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I wouldn’t say I feel worthless exactly, or at least that word feels a little strong, but guilty, yes. Definitely guilty. And it can be over things that, when I say them out loud, sound kind of small.

Like with the arts board stuff, that’s really why I came in. Since I started trying to sort of clean up my image and get my routine in better shape, I thought I’d feel more on top of things, but instead I’ve been more tense and down than I expected. I’m still doing the basics, so it’s not like everything has fallen apart, but I keep letting these little things slip that I normally care about — fair planning notes, design drafts, those kinds of things.

And then once I’ve let something sit too long, it weighs on me way more than it probably should. I’ll think, “Why didn’t I just do that earlier?” or “Now it’s going to look like I don’t care,” and then I feel guilty enough that I avoid people or cancel plans instead of just catching up. Which is stupid, because canceling doesn’t even mean I actually get the work done. Sometimes I just sit there feeling bad about not doing the work and also feeling bad about canceling.

So, yes, the guilt part is there. It’s not constant self-hatred or anything, but it does really sit on me and make small things feel bigger than they are.
